David Mařák, CTO and Co-Founder of YORD – Interview Series

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

David Mařák is CTO and Co-Founder of YORD. With over a decade of experience in immersive technologies, he leads the studio’s technology strategy and development at the intersection of artificial intelligence, extended reality, spatial computing, and interactive digital experiences. His work spans AI-powered applications, virtual and augmented reality, digital humans, and real-time generative technologies.

YORD is a creative technology studio that designs and develops AI and XR experiences for brands and organizations. Its work includes interactive installations, immersive brand experiences, museum environments, training solutions, and custom digital products, combining creative development with emerging technologies.

YORD builds for Apple Vision Pro and other headsets. How do Samsung’s glasses compare from a developer’s perspective, and what do they mean for immersive retail?

Vision Pro is a closed, highly optimized device with a futuristic look, and as a developer, you know exactly what you’re working with. Samsung’s glasses are built to look like normal glasses, and Android XR will run on hardware from several manufacturers, so it’s more open and varied, and not perfectly tuned for any one device.

A few years ago I’d have seen that fragmentation as a big problem. Today I don’t think it matters that much. With AI in the development process, it’s a lot easier to optimize for a specific device, read through someone’s documentation, or port between platforms, so the open platform tends to win because more people build for it. In the long run I’d bet on Android XR for that reason. For immersive retail though, there’s nothing on these glasses right now. No display means we can’t build anything the customer would see.

Is there any retail interaction that becomes possible once people wear these glasses in a store, something a phone can’t do?

Not yet. I should say that I’m a fan of glasses in general. We’ve been living digital lives through our phones for 15 years, it’s just not comfortable, you have to look down at a small rectangle and hold it in one hand the whole time. Glasses are the natural next step.

But Samsung’s glasses don’t solve that. You can look at a product and ask Gemini about it without getting your phone out, which is nice, but the phone can already do the same thing; the camera has just moved to your nose. The interaction that would be new is looking at a product and seeing the price, material, reviews or stock availability directly on the shelf. Samsung’s glasses can recognize the product, and that’s where it ends.

What’s the biggest limitation of a display-free device for brands, and what should they do until display versions arrive?

You can’t show anything. You can have great product data, and a clever assistant behind it, and the customer still gets a spoken description instead of seeing the thing.

I wouldn’t build any immersive strategy around these glasses. If a brand wants to do XR, do it on hardware that supports XR today, and keep the content in a format that can move to other platforms later. Good 3D models of your products, clean product data, experiences that aren’t locked to one device. When light glasses with a real display arrive, and I think they will, that’s what you’ll be able to reuse.

Warby Parker stores are the demo site. Why does a physical store make sense for this launch?

I don’t see it as a testing ground so much as the place where the glasses get sold. They’re prescription glasses with a camera in them, and prescription glasses get sold at the optician. You try them on, they fit your lenses, you walk out wearing them. Samsung and Google needed a chain with hundreds of stores and their own optical labs, and Warby Parker is that. Meta did the same thing with Ray-Ban and EssilorLuxottica.

I think it’s the right call. Glasses are a fashion item and a medical device before they’re a gadget, and selling them in an electronics store would probably not work. It also tells you what the product is. The frames are what’s for sale, and Gemini is something you get with them.

Camera glasses make people uncomfortable. How should brands deal with that in stores and public spaces?

I don’t think the camera itself is the issue. We walk around with a phone camera in our hand all day, and nobody calls that a surveillance device. The glasses just move a camera you already have to a different place.

The difference is that with a phone you can see when someone is pointing it at something, and with glasses the people around you can’t tell. So, at minimum, there has to be a recording light that is bright, visible from the front, and can’t be turned off, and I’d prefer that to be a platform rule rather than something each manufacturer decides. Then there’s the question of what gets recorded, how long it’s stored, who has access to it, and what developers like us can see. Samsung and Google haven’t said much about any of that. Until they do, I wouldn’t build in-store experiences that rely on continuously watching the customer.

For retailers who want to do something useful today, what’s the low-hanging fruit?

Making existing product information available through voice, and then testing whether anyone actually prefers it to taking out their phone. I don’t see much more than that.

For any glasses project, the test is whether it does something better than what the customer already has in their pocket. If the same thing works just as well on a phone, there’s no reason to pay for a glasses version. If a retailer wants a real immersive experience, that’s possible today on other hardware, and we can build that. Trying to do XR on a device with no display will end up being a gimmick.

YORD works across many platforms. How does that shape the advice you give brands about investing in XR?

The main thing we’ve learned is not to mistake a hardware announcement for a development opportunity. Vision Pro, Quest and the others are quite different, but on all of them we can build something visual that reacts to the user’s surroundings. On Samsung’s glasses, we can’t, so before asking how to port an experience, you have to ask whether the device can run it at all.

Otherwise the advice hasn’t changed. Don’t tie your XR plans to a single manufacturer. We’ve been through enough hardware generations to know the devices will look different in two years. The content you’ve built and the users you’ve reached are what stays with you.

How do you measure whether an XR experience builds loyalty, compared to a normal digital touchpoint?

I wouldn’t count how many people tried it, because everyone tries new hardware once. I’d look at what happened afterward. Did they understand the product better? Did they decide faster? Did they buy? Did they come back? These are the same metrics you’d use for a website or an app, and the XR version has to beat them; otherwise, there’s no reason to do it.

With real XR, there’s a decent chance it does, because you’re showing someone the product in their own space rather than on a screen. With glasses that have no display, I’d be careful about promising anything like that. Measure it against a phone app and see what comes out.

What’s the biggest misconception brands have about XR right now, and does Samsung’s launch change it?

That anything with glasses, a headset, or AI in it is XR. It isn’t. XR refers to digital content that’s tied to the physical space around you, so the device has to understand the environment and be able to change what you see.

Google Glass had a display but almost no understanding of what you were looking at. Samsung’s glasses are the opposite; they understand the surroundings through the camera and Gemini but can’t show you anything. Each has one half of it. A few years ago, the industry put the metaverse label on a lot of things that had nothing to do with immersive computing, and I’m a bit worried we’re about to do the same with AI glasses. Samsung’s product might be a good wearable assistant. It’s not an XR device just because the name says so.

As a CTO, what are you watching over the next 6 to 12 months to tell whether XR glasses are heading for the mass market or staying niche?

Two things: whether the hardware gets there and whether developers get access.

On hardware, I think glasses will eventually replace the phone, because everything we do on a phone we’d rather do without holding it. What’s missing is a single device that has a see-through display, understands the space around you, and comes with a frame you can wear from morning to evening. Vision Pro, Galaxy XR or Quest are too heavy and too expensive for that. Samsung’s glasses are light enough but can’t show anything. Snap’s Specs, which shipped this autumn for around $2,200, are the closest so far. Still too expensive and a bit thick, but they have a real display, they understand the room, and we can develop for them. That’s the direction I’d expect things to go, and Samsung is currently behind it.

On developers, I want to see whether we’ll be able to ship our own apps, render spatial content, and work with the data the device uses to understand the environment, and whether the camera rules get written down. If the platforms open up, people will build on them quickly because development has become much cheaper with AI. If they stay closed around one assistant, we’ll end up with a slightly better Alexa.

Thank you for the great interview, readers who wish to explore how XR, AI, and spatial computing can create useful retail and brand experiences should visit YORD.

As a futurist, he is dedicated to exploring how these innovations will shape our world. In addition, he is the founder of Securities.io, a platform focused on investing in cutting-edge technologies that are redefining the future and reshaping entire sectors.

Expo Opens Paid-Plan Access to Cloud Simulators for AI Coding Agents

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Expo announced on November 12, 2026 that Expo Simulators is available to everyone on its paid plans, giving AI coding agents cloud iOS simulators and Android emulators on which to run and verify app changes. Usage costs $0.10 per simulator minute on either platform.

The paid-plan launch expands the limited-access EAS Simulator preview Expo introduced on September 14, 2026. An Expo blog post dated November 10, 2026 was still directing developers to the waitlist, making broader paid-plan availability the new step rather than the first public demonstration of cloud devices.

Expo’s premise is that code generation has shifted the bottleneck to verification, with agents producing changes faster than engineering teams can test and review them. Mobile adds a hardware constraint: iOS simulators run only on Apple hardware, while cloud agents typically run on Linux. An agent that cannot run the app has to assess code without seeing the resulting behavior.

The local alternative also consumes developer-machine capacity. In Expo’s example, seven agents working in parallel can require seven simulators or emulators, and Android emulators can each use 4 GB or more of RAM. Remote sessions shift that compute to Expo infrastructure and let teams run an iOS simulator from Linux, Windows through the Windows Subsystem for Linux, or a cloud sandbox without a local Mac.

The intended loop starts with a bug report in a tool such as Linear. An agent reproduces the issue, writes a fix and tests it on cloud devices in parallel, away from the developer’s machine. It can place a screen recording, screenshots and logs on the pull request, while teammates open the running build from a link without requiring a TestFlight build. The service works with Cursor, Claude Code and Codex, and adds no AI model of its own: Expo is supplying the device runtime and evidence rather than another coding model.

Expo co-founder and CTO James Ide described the intended review model: “The agent was the fast part. Expo Simulators gives every agent a simulator, so a fix arrives for review already tested on several simulators, and the only decision left is whether it’s the right code. Watch it run, then merge.”

A session can be started through EAS CLI, the REST API or an authenticated creation link, according to the Expo Simulators documentation. The session can install an Expo Build, a build whose native code matches the project’s fingerprint, an application archive or Expo Go; without an application source, it starts with a blank device. Agents and test jobs can control apps through agent-device, Argent or Appium, while a browser preview lets people watch the run. The official Expo Simulators skill packages access checks, build preparation, session limits, device control, evidence collection and cleanup for agents.

The product page lists support for React Native, Swift, Kotlin, Flutter, Unity and other frameworks. Apps do not have to be built with Expo or React Native, but the build must run on an iOS Simulator or Android Emulator and the project must be linked to Expo Services. Each session runs in an isolated environment with session-scoped credentials and access-controlled previews, and stopping it disables the simulator and controller endpoints.

On Expo’s pricing page, the Starter, Production and Enterprise plans each include 60 simulator minutes per month until 2027, with usage-based pricing beyond that allowance. Paid sessions can run for up to 115 minutes.

Parallelism does not create unlimited account capacity. The usage and billing guide says simulator sessions draw on the account’s job concurrency and queue when no capacity is free. Ending a CI job or returning from a non-interactive command also does not stop the remote device. Teams must stop it explicitly or rely on a duration or idle limit; otherwise, an unattended session continues consuming usage. That makes cleanup and session limits part of cost control for automated workflows.

The limitations guide adds three material qualifications: the simulator CLI commands remain experimental, the service does not provide physical devices, and recordings may miss frames and therefore cannot prove or disprove a brief frame drop. A cloud runtime can verify app behavior, but it does not eliminate the need for physical-device testing or profiling where those tools are required.

Expo’s next disclosed step is Agent Sessions, a planned enterprise layer for triggering agents from sources such as customer feedback and protecting against prompt injection, where malicious input could direct an agent to expose data. Expo expects to share details by the end of November 2026; this is a roadmap item rather than a capability included in the current launch.

Simulators adds a verification stage to Expo’s existing build, CI/CD, app-store submission, over-the-air update and monitoring services. The result is a division of labor rather than an autonomous release process: agents supply tested changes and runtime evidence, account limits govern how much runs at once, and people retain the merge decision.

Unlock expands home equity agreements to Georgia

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Unlock Technologies expanded its home equity agreement product to Georgia, bringing availability to more than half of U.S. states. Unlock cited $248,000 in average home equity for Georgia homeowners in Q1 2026 and a 37.7% equity-rich share in Q2 2026.  AI Summary

Unlock Technologies announced on Thursday that it has expanded its home equity agreement (HEA) product to Georgia, bringing its availability to more than half of U.S. states.

More than 25,000 homeowners nationwide have used an Unlock HEA to access their home equity, the Tempe, Arizona-based company said in its release.

The agreements allow homeowners to receive cash in exchange for a percentage of their home’s future value without taking on an additional monthly payment. Homeowners retain ownership and can continue living in their homes. They also avoid refinancing an existing mortgage, which can be advantageous in a higher-rate environment.

Georgia homeowners with a mortgage held an average of $248,000 in home equity at the end of the first quarter of 2026, according to Cotality data cited by Unlock. Separately, 37.7% of mortgaged homes in the state were considered equity-rich in the second quarter, meaning homeowners owed no more than half of their property’s estimated market value, according to ATTOM data cited in the announcement.

Georgia was the second-highest state for inbound interest on Unlock.com among states where the company had not yet launched, said Michael Micheletti, Unlock’s chief marketing officer. “That level of interest reinforces what we’re seeing across the country: homeowners want greater flexibility in how they access and use their home equity to address critical life needs,” he said.

“Homeownership is getting more expensive, and Georgia homeowners are feeling the impact of rising property taxes, insurance premiums and maintenance costs,” Micheletti added. “Many homeowners have built substantial equity, but their monthly budgets don’t always reflect that. Unlock gives homeowners a way to access that equity without adding another monthly payment.”

Zero-down VA loans help push veteran homeownership to 77%, study finds

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

This article analyzes new 2024 ACS data showing veterans have a 77% homeownership rate versus 60% for non-veterans, with especially large advantages for buyers under 35 in markets like Memphis, Fresno and Virginia Beach.   AI Summary

Veterans own homes at much higher rates than non-veterans, a gap driven in part by access to specialized mortgage programs that are also helping narrow racial homeownership disparities, according to an analysis of 2024 American Community Survey data for the nation’s 50 largest metro areas.

The veteran homeownership rate was 77% in 2024, compared to 60% for non-veterans. Among adults under 35, 46.6% of veterans were homeowners versus 31.9% of non-veterans, showing that military service is associated with earlier entry into homeownership.

Homeownership remains the primary path to wealth for U.S. households. Federal Reserve data show the median wealth of a typical homeowner is more than 40 times the median wealth of a typical renter, underscoring why access to mortgage credit and affordable payments is central to closing wealth gaps.

Among the 50 largest metro areas, Virginia Beach, Virginia, has both the highest concentration of veterans and the largest homeownership advantage for that population. In Virginia Beach, 80% of veterans own homes, compared with 57.4% of non-veterans.

Other large metros where veterans significantly outperform civilians on homeownership include:

Veterans also see a notable edge in high-cost coastal markets where civilian homeownership is constrained by high prices. In places like New York, San Diego and Fresno, access to 0% down payment financing through the VA loan program can provide a substantial relative boost for eligible buyers compared to civilians reliant on conventional financing.

The homeownership advantage is particularly pronounced for veterans under 35. In Memphis, 66% of young veterans are homeowners, compared with 46.9% of young non-veterans, a 19.1 percentage point gap.

Other top metros for young veteran homeownership include:

While younger veterans still have lower homeownership rates than older veterans, they are substantially more likely to own homes than their non-veteran peers. For lenders and real estate agents, that indicates a distinct demand segment for first-time buyer products and education tailored to veteran households.

The analysis also finds that veteran status is associated with reduced racial disparities in homeownership. Across the 50 largest metros, overall homeownership rates are 72.1% for white households and 49.5% for non-white households, reflecting a long history of segregation and discrimination in housing.

Among veteran households, however, the white-nonwhite homeownership gap shrinks to 13.6 percentage points, as military service appears to elevate non-white homeownership rates.

Several large metros show particularly small racial gaps among veterans:

For policymakers and housing practitioners focused on equity, these patterns suggest that veteran-specific benefits and underwriting frameworks may offer lessons for reducing racial gaps in the broader market.

Veterans benefit from a web of federal, state and nonprofit housing resources, with the U.S. Department of Veterans Affairs’ purchase loan program at the core. In fiscal year 2025, the VA guaranteed 323,835 purchase loans, according to the analysis.

VA purchase loans allow eligible veterans and service members to buy with zero down payment and no ongoing monthly mortgage insurance. Because the loans are backed by a partial federal guarantee, they typically feature lower interest rates, capped closing costs and more flexible qualification standards than conventional loans.

Beyond VA loans, veterans may qualify for:

Yet many eligible veterans are not fully aware of these benefits or how to access them. For lenders, servicers and real estate agents, that awareness gap represents both a business opportunity and an avenue to support wealth-building and housing stability among veteran households.

For mortgage lenders, veteran households — especially those under 35 — represent a resilient and often under-served segment of potential homebuyers. Understanding VA eligibility rules, pricing structures and documentation requirements is critical to capturing this demand and ensuring borrowers are matched with the most advantageous products.

Real estate agents who can navigate VA appraisal, property condition standards and seller perceptions around VA offers are better positioned to guide veteran clients in competitive markets, particularly in high-cost coastal metros where the relative advantage of VA financing is largest.

Housing nonprofits and local governments can leverage this data to target outreach and counseling in metros where veterans cluster and where racial homeownership gaps among veterans are already narrower, using those markets as benchmarks for broader equity strategies.

How You Collaborate With AI on Creative Work Makes All the Difference

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

A new study dives into the consequences of collaborating with AI on creative endeavors, and what it means for output and satisfaction at work.

Did you use AI at your job today? Maybe it helped you work faster. But too much AI may reduce both the quality of your work and your satisfaction with the job, according to a study published Nov. 10 in the PNAS Nexus science journal.

The team behind the study knew from prior research that generative AI was a mixed bag, capable of dramatically boosting productivity, but also prone to throttling creative diversity and worker satisfaction.

“The question we wanted to answer was whether these trade-offs are inevitable,” Kartik Hosanagar, a University of Pennsylvania professor of technology, digital business and marketing, and one of the study’s authors, told CNET via email. “Our research suggests they aren’t.”

AI is changing work quickly, with dizzying contradictions. Tech companies like Microsoft and Meta are spending millions on integrating AI into their business for greater efficiencies and output, while getting rid of human workers through mass buyouts and layoffs. Workers who use AI report some advantages, like saving 40 to 60 minutes a day at work, while suffering consequences like loss of confidence, loss of skills and a tougher labor market.

Teachers have also sounded the alarm, saying AI is essentially ending homework. Researchers say that if we don’t pay attention to how we collaborate with AI, we could start losing critical thinking along the way.

“Think about GPS navigation. It’s incredibly useful, but many of us have lost the ability to navigate even familiar neighborhoods without it,” Hosanagar said. “Something similar could happen with AI and cognitive skills.”

The study by Hosanagar’s team focused on the different ways we collaborate with AI on creative tasks. They found that the way we use AI — not just whether or not we use it — has a big effect on the final product and on our perception of the work.

“The future of work isn’t just about how capable AI becomes,” Hosanagar said, “but how we design the collaboration between humans and AI so as to support a broader set of goals.”

The researchers tasked 285 students, split into three groups, with writing assignments over two days. On the first day, no students used AI for writing. On the second day, each group used a different process for writing with AI.

Each of the groups used a separate “collaboration design” when working with the AI on the writing assignment. The collaboration designs represent different ways people interact with AI on the job. All three approaches delivered similar productivity benefits, but differed significantly in quality, satisfaction and creative diversity, according to Hosanagar.

In the first group, Human Confirmation, the AI planned, outlined, wrote and edited the assignment, with the humans just approving the work during each step. In the second, Human Creativity, humans did the outlining and planning, while AI did the writing and editing. In the third, Copilot, humans and AI collaborated during the entire process.

The researchers found that using AI in creative writing did not improve the quality of the work in any of the groups, but it did speed up the task.

People in the Human Confirmation produced the lowest quality and least diverse work, and participants reported feeling unsatisfied with it. But the other two groups, Human Creativity and Copilot, produced better work at the same speed, with participants reporting a more positive experience.

The study showed that the best outcomes happen when humans are actively involved in creative tasks, especially generating ideas and creating outlines, while AI assists with execution, Hosanagar said. “The goal shouldn’t be to eliminate human effort, but to direct that effort toward the parts of the process where human judgment and creativity matter most.”

It would be good to be wary, too, of the unintended consequences of people playing a smaller role in their daily work as they collaborate with AI.

“Our research also suggests that people derive satisfaction from having a meaningful creative role, not simply from producing a good final output,” Hosanagar said. “So, we should be thoughtful about what we automate.”

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/providers/bon-secours-mercy-health-acquire-majority-stake-surgery-partners" hreflang="en">Bon Secours Mercy Health bumps up offer for majority stake in Surgery Partners to $6B valuation</a>

📅 2026-11-12  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →
Bon Secours Mercy Health bumps up offer for majority stake in Surgery Partners to $6B valuation" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Bon Secours Mercy Health bumped the price tag on its pending transaction to acquire a majority share in healthcare services company Surgery Partners in response to an unsolicited offer the short-stay surgical platform received from a third party, the organizations shared this week.

The transaction now values the acquiree at $6 billion, or $25 per share, up from the initial $5.7 billion and $23.50 per share (see that story below). Additionally, the parties agreed to an additional $1.61 per share in ticking fees if the deal doesn't close by March 1, 2027.

The companies' announcement does not name the third party, which offered $28 per share in a Nov. 3 proposal. A report from Bloomberg, citing an unnamed source, names UnitedHealth as the extra bidder whose offer was rejected over concerns about deal complexity and ability to close.

Surgery Partners’ business platform includes ambulatory surgery centers, surgical hospitals and about 5,000 affiliated physicians spanning 30 states. It reported $3.31 billion in revenue during 2025 and $0.47 adjusted earnings per share.

Affiliates of Bain Capital Private Equity currently control about 39% of Surgery Partners. Under the current deal, Bain would roll over its shares while the remainder would be acquired by a Bon Secours affiliate, HealthSpan Partners.

Bon Secours Mercy Health is acquiring a majority share in healthcare services company Surgery Partners in a take-private deal valuing the acquiree at $5.7 billion, the organizations announced Monday morning.

The transaction is expected to close in the first quarter of next year, pending approvals, and with over 200 locations in tow, would help the major nonprofit health system create “one of the country’s largest outpatient surgical care platforms,” they said.

“As healthcare continues to evolve, it is important to grow in ways that allow us to serve more people, in more communities and through a broader range of care settings,” John M. Starcher, Jr., president and CEO of Bon Secours Mercy Health, said in the announcement. “Our relationship with Surgery Partners represents an important step toward advancing that commitment."

Currently, affiliates of Bain Capital Private Equity control about 39% of the public Surgery Partners. Under the terms of the deal, Bain Capital would retain and roll over those shares, while HealthSpan Partners, an affiliate of Bon Secours, would purchase the remainder with cash at $23.50 per share.

Surgery Partners’ short-stay surgical platform includes ambulatory surgery centers, surgical hospitals and about 5,000 affiliated physicians spanning 30 states. It reported $3.31 billion in revenue during 2025 and $0.47 adjusted earnings per share.

The company has been public since 2015, with a 2017 merger making it the largest standalone, independent surgical facilities operator at the time. Bain Capital made an offer in January 2025 to fully acquire the shares it didn’t own for $25.75 apiece (roughly $6.7 billion enterprise valuation), though that offer was rejected a few months later.

Surgery Partners has also been slimming down some of its acute care holdings in recent months, a strategic move its leadership said would better position the company as a purely short-stay surgical provider while limiting its exposure to Medicaid.

“We believe this transaction represents the best path forward for Surgery Partners, delivering an attractive all-cash premium to our shareholders while creating lasting benefits for our patients, physicians and other stakeholders,” Surgery Partners CEO Eric Evans said Monday.

Bon Secours Mercy Health is a Catholic nonprofit with 47 hospitals, more than 1,200 care sites and nearly 60,000 employees. It reported just shy of $14 billion in total operating revenue through 2025, as well as $324 million of recurring operating income (2.3% recurring operating margin) and a $1.1 billion bottom line. Of note, its holdings include 41% ownership in revenue cycle management company Ensemble Health Partners, for which a summer strategic recapitalization transaction with a new private equity investor injected $670.8 million in cash into the system, per recent financial statements.

Bon Secours’ HealthSpan Partners is financing the Surgery Partners purchase with a combination of cash on hand and newly secured debt. Upon closing, the parties plan to keep Surgery Partners’ operations, brand and headquarters in place, while also keeping the business as a secular organization.

Strategically, the deal is evidence of large health systems’ growing investments in ambulatory care networks, where many expect to see rising demand as consumers prioritize lower-cost care settings. Healthcare executives have bluntly described these investments as a “top priority,” particularly as regulatory and policy activity such as the wind-down of Medicare’s inpatient-only list pushes care outside of the hospital.

“As more care moves beyond the hospital setting, patients are looking for high-quality care that fits more seamlessly into their lives,” Wael Haidar, M.D., Bon Secours Mercy Health’s president of clinical operations, said in the announcement. “Surgery Partners understands that care should be designed around the needs of patients, and that approach aligns closely with our mission. Together, we have an opportunity to expand access to high-quality outpatient care while supporting physicians and helping patients receive the right care, in the right setting, at the right time.”

The most apparent analogue for Bon Secours’ deal comes from Ascension, an even larger Catholic nonprofit, which closed a $3.9 billion deal over the summer to acquire AMSURG, an ambulatory surgery management services company with over 300 locations. Ascension CEO Eduardo Conrado has described that deal as both a means to flesh out his system’s existing markets—improving overall volumes and allowing hospitals to focus on high-complexity patients—and a way to strike new business partnerships with other local health systems in markets beyond its prior footprint.

How Kelly Rowland Keeps Her Eczema Flare-Ups Under Control During Winter

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

Wintertime can bring everyone down with its colder weather and less sunlight. But for those with eczema, this time of year can wreak havoc on their skin, too.

That’s the case for Grammy-winning singer, actress, and Destiny’s Child alum Kelly Rowland, who has been living with eczema since her 20s. During the winter months, she often dreads the cold.

“[You] don’t know what the environment is going to give you—because mine is also environmental—or what the weather is going to give you,” Rowland told Healthline.

Cold weather and indoor heating can reduce humidity, drying out the skin and triggering or worsening eczema flare-ups.

“[What we know] about the skin barrier is that it really is like a gate or wall against the outside world,” Camille Howard-Verovic, MD, a dermatologist at Active Dermatology, told Healthline.

In healthy skin, the barrier controls what enters and leaves the skin. However, for people with eczema, Howard-Verovic said they have skin barrier dysfunction, which allows substances such as allergens and irritants to penetrate the skin.

Cold weather can increase transepidermal water loss, a process in which moisture escapes through the skin’s outer barrier and evaporates into the surrounding air.

“People with eczema have a tremendous amount of transepidermal water loss, so you’re just drier,” said Howard-Verovic. “[You’re] not holding onto the water as well, so that causes the itching and the rash that we feel when we have eczema.”

Rowland first noticed eczema flares behind her ears as well as on her elbows, arms, and the back of her knees.

“[It] looked like a rash, so that’s what I thought it was all this time, and I dealt with it for so long until after I had my son and then it started to get worse on my face,” she said.

She eventually went to a dermatologist who diagnosed her with moderate-to-severe eczema, a chronic inflammatory skin condition that can cause intense itching, painful skin cracking, thickened skin, and redness or swelling.

When Rowland learned that eczema can be genetic, it made sense to her.

“I remember seeing big rashes on [my mom’s] neck and her arms…so she had it, and I guess I was the lucky one,” said Rowland.

At first, she tried eliminating foods that she thought might be triggers. She also bathed in oatmeal.

“[I] literally have had a tub full of oatmeal, like literally went to the store to get store-bought fresh oats,” she said. “I tried everything, and it doesn’t help until you go talk to a doctor about it.”

At first, she tried prescription topical medications but found them cumbersome.

“I don’t have the time, if I’m being honest, and we live in a world that is moving so fast…I started buying like three tubes of whatever it was I was using and losing it; it was just too much for me,” Rowland said.

Rowland said she found relief with Ebglyss, a prescription injectable medication for moderate-to-severe atopic dermatitis. She has also partnered with Eli Lilly and Company, its manufacturer, to raise awareness about eczema.

Rowland said she’s sharing her experience to help others living with eczema, including people she’s met who have experienced greater challenges managing the condition.

“One lady was telling me about how she’s having a tough time [looking] in the mirror cause she doesn’t know who’s looking back at her, and she saw her skin before eczema and after,” said Rowland.

When she first had symptoms, she said she felt embarrassed, too.

“[Because] it was just in the middle of my arm space and…[if I] put out my hand to shake someone’s hand, you don’t know if somebody’s judgy,” she said.

Rowland hopes being open about her condition will encourage others to advocate for themselves and talk with their doctor about treatment options.

Because eczema can look different on deeper skin tones, Howard-Verovic noted that a dermatologist can help make the correct diagnosis.

“Some people may not even know that they have eczema,” she said. “We know that eczema is a rash; it can appear red on certain skin tones, and it can be grey or white on certain skin tones, so it can look a little different.”

In addition to medication, Rowland said managing stress helps reduce her eczema flare-ups.

“Before [when] I went on tour, because I was under so much stress, it flared up like crazy, like you wouldn’t believe,” she said. “We were in the back trying to figure out how to cover certain things up, and we figured some of it out, and some of it was really tricky, but what helped was when I actually just calmed down…not that it helped overnight, but it did just kind of bring the flare down.”

She said red light therapy, feeling grounded outside without her shoes on, getting vitamin D from natural sunlight, and cooking are ways she reduces ongoing stress.

“Cooking calms me when my kids aren’t here, and I have a really good [Zen] playlist on,” said Rowland. “[If] you ask me a question [in those moments], I might give you my heart; I’m just so happy.”

She also tries to cut back on sugar, which she believes triggers flares.

Another way to reduce flares, according to Howard-Verovic, is to avoid hot showers, especially in the winter.

“[We] know eczematous skin likes lukewarm water,” she said.

Because Howard-Verovic and her daughter both live with eczema, she set a rule about when it’s time to get out of the shower.

“[If] you see condensation on the bathroom mirror, you’ve spent too much time in the shower,” said Howard-Verovic. “[That] really hot, hot water feels really good in that moment…but when you get out all that water evaporates, and there’s no moisture in the atmosphere, and it dries your skin out, and now your skin is unhappy again.”

If you prefer to take baths, she said use gentle cleansers.

“[There] are bleach baths for really, really bad flare-ups,” she noted.

Diluted bleach baths are sometimes recommended for people with eczema, but they should only be used according to a healthcare professional’s instructions.

As soon as you get out of the shower or bath, Howard-Verovic said to apply a quality moisturizer to your damp skin. According to the National Eczema Association (NEA), lotions containing the following ingredients can help ease dry skin associated with eczema.

To help identify appropriate products, look for ones that have the NEA’s Seal of Acceptance.

Google’s Pixel Tag tracker won’t work as well on older Android phones – check our list

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Tech > Services & Software > Operating Systems > Mobile OS > Android

Several of the tracker’s best features require Bluetooth 6.0-compatible device.

Google’s Pixel Tag, the company’s take on an Android-exclusive Bluetooth tracker, is finally here, following its August announcement during the Google I/O developer conference.

Also: Google’s new Android XR glasses have lower ‘creep factor’ than competitors

Pixel Tag is a complete Bluetooth tracker for Android users that appears less confined to device-specific ecosystems than Samsung’s Smart Tag 3 and Apple’s AirTag.

However, if you look at Google’s footnotes, the Pixel Tag’s functionality is diminished when an older Android phone is involved. Several of the tracker’s best features require a compatible device running Android 9.0+ and Bluetooth 6.0+, according to Google.

Android 9 and newer encompasses a lot of devices, but Bluetooth 6.0 and newer doesn’t. Here’s what to check before you buy.

Smartphones began shipping with Bluetooth 6.0 in 2025, starting with the Samsung Galaxy S26 Plus and Galaxy S26 Ultra. The following phones also have Bluetooth 6.0:

If your Android phone is older than any of the listed above, even by a generation, it likely supports Bluetooth 5.3 or 5.4 — not enough for the Pixel Tag’s best features.

The Pixel Tag’s precision-finding capabilities are powered by Bluetooth Channel Sounding, a key feature introduced in Bluetooth 6.0. Nearly every review I’ve seen of the Pixel Tag, including our own, tests the tracker when paired with a Pixel 10, Pixel 10 Pro, Pixel 11, or Pixel 11 Pro.

I assume that Android users whose phones run Android 9.0+ but lack Bluetooth 6.0+ can use the Pixel Tag for very basic, general location tracking. These users may see the tracker’s general location, but Google’s device compatibility footnote appears several times throughout its press release.

The following features require Android 9.0+ and Bluetooth 6.0+, according to Google’s release:

So, on older Android devices without Android 9.0+ and Bluetooth 6.0+, the following aren’t supported: locating the exact direction and distance of your tracked devices, pinging a tracker when you don’t see it, or giving your tracked devices a custom name.

Also: 3 ways Bluetooth 7 upgrades your devices beyond lossless audio

Additionally, shorter-distance tracking requires an ultra-wideband chip inside your Android phone. Channel Sounding handles distance and direction at longer ranges, while UWB takes over for shorter ranges. Most premium Android phones with Bluetooth 6.0 also have UWB chips, but several of Google’s base-model phones lack a UWB chip.

Samsung’s base-model, A-series, and Z Flip-series phones also lack UWB, resulting in more phones that can’t access the Pixel Tag’s short-range precision finding capabilities.

With all the specs in mind, the Pixel Tag is the best option for users of recent premium flagship Android phones. Those without the latest Bluetooth connectivity will find that the Pixel Tag works like any other Bluetooth tracker, with basic functionality.

Also: These are the closest things to AirTags for Android users

The good thing is that a single Pixel Tag is $30, so it’s not much more expensive than a Tile Tag, and cheaper than a Chipolo Loop. Still, if your phone doesn’t support Bluetooth 6.0, you might be better off looking for a cheaper option until you’re ready to upgrade.

Better 2.0 plan hits reported $34.1M in cost savings

Better Home & Finance Holding Co. announced on Tuesday that it has reached $34.1 million in annualized cost savings as it continues a turnaround under its Better 2.0 plan — which includes eliminating management and loan support positions while replacing some duties with artificial intelligence.

The latest figure, reported in the company’s fifth weekly scorecard, represents progress toward a $64.2 million cost-reduction target. Better also added five brokerages to its wholesale platform, advanced financing negotiations tied to the planned sale of its U.K. bank subsidiary and hired executive search firm Daversa Partners to lead its CEO search.

The additional $3.7 million in annualized savings reported this week comes from further reductions in mortgage operations and the corporate organization. Better previously reported $30.4 million in savings during the first two weeks of its turnaround initiative.

The latest scorecard, which is released each Tuesday until the company reaches adjusted EBITDA breakeven, follows Better’s third-quarter earnings reported on Monday. Founder Vishal Garg detailed additional workforce reductions, including the elimination of management layers and loan support roles that he said would be permanently replaced by AI tools.

During the quarter, the company recorded a net loss of $36.5 million, compared with a $30.6 million loss in the second quarter and a $39.1 million loss in the same period last year. The company originated $1.4 billion in loans during the quarter, up 15% year over year.

Better has removed managers representing more than $3.5 million in annualized savings, Garg told analysts. The company also eliminated its home lending associate position, which handled early customer outreach and application preparation before transferring borrowers to loan officers. Garg confirmed that these responsibilities are now handled by Better’s AI system, Betsy.

In the latest scorecard, Better said its wholesale platform now includes 43 brokerages, representing 1,382 brokers and $378 million in trailing 12-month origination volume. That compares with 39 signed brokerages reported during the company’s earnings call.

Better also launched its AI-powered home equity line of credit (HELOC) product with a large existing partner. The company plans to complete live customer testing before ramping up to full partnership volume. It is also working with three other major partners and pursuing a revived large-scale partnership.

On the capital markets front, Better received five term sheets for approximately $50 million in debt financing tied to its planned sale of Birmingham Bank. The company is negotiating terms with prospective financing partners.

As for Better’s CEO search with Daversa Partners, the next steps outlined in the scorecard include finalizing the candidate profile and beginning outreach.

World’s first ‘dark magic mushroom’ discovered in Florida

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Science  |  Read Source →

Published Nov 12, 2026 11:59 AM EST

A mushroom recently discovered in Florida looks unassuming, but this fungus

packs a serious and surprising punch. According to mycologists writing in the journal Current Biology, Galerina indica is the first-known “dark magic mushroom,” a species featuring hefty doses of both psychedelic and poisonous compounds.

Depending on their chemical makeup, many mushrooms will do one of two things. Fungi packed with psilocybin can send users on an extensive psychedelic trip, while varieties laden with amatoxins can be poisonous. Although mycologists have long assumed a single species could hypothetically feature both traits, no one had ever documented such an example. A recent field survey changed that, however.

“I could hardly believe it. We knew it was possible, but tried not to get too excited until we did a more rigorous analysis,” Andrew Kunik, a former University of Florida plant pathologist and study co-author, said in a statement.

Evolutionary biologists suspect certain mushrooms developed their poisonous amatoxins as a protection against foraging animals, although hallucinogenic psilocybin’s emergence and purpose in fungi remains unclear. Regardless, both compounds found their way into G. indica. University of Utah mycologist and study co-author Bryn Dentinger believes that a process called horizontal gene transfer is likely to blame—although it’s a completely novel discovery.

“Horizontal gene transfer is like if we shook hands and some of my DNA incorporated into your genome,” he said. “[But] two independent horizontal gene transfer events for these specialized metabolites in one mushroom genus are unheard of.”

For as unique as G. indica is, it is very easy to miss. The small, innocuous brown mushrooms are barely the size of a thumbnail, and resemble countless other fungi.

“These mushrooms are really hard to find,” added study co-author, University of Florida plant pathologist, and state mycologist Matthew Smith. “It’s hard to differentiate them from others, even for a trained expert.”

Although difficult, documenting the specimens is not only vital to the field of mycology. It can help save lives.

“Part of my job as Florida’s state mycologist is to identify poisonous or potentially poisonous species,” explained Smith. “So it is important to know what species we have and whether they might cause problems for people or pets.”

Existing home sales hit a new low as rates stay high

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Existing home sales slowed to a 3.75 million annual pace in October, down 5.5% MoM and 8.8% YoY, with declines across all regions. Inventory increased to 1.59 million and the median price rose 0.4% YoY to $416,700.  AI Summary

The annual pace of existing home sales continues to decline compared to a year ago. In October, existing home sales slowed to an annual pace of 3.75 million, according to data published on Thursday by the National Association of Realtors (NAR).

This represents a 5.5% decline from a month ago and an 8.8% drop from October 2025.

“Home buying is difficult when mortgage rates have climbed to their highest levels since 2000, aside from a single month in 2023. As a result, home sales fell to their lowest level since August 2010,” Lawrence Yun,” NAR’s chief economist, said in a statement.

HousingWire Data shows similar trends, noting that for the week ending November 6, 2026, an estimated 74,473 single family homes were sold, down 4.5% compared to a year ago. In addition, the median sales price declined 3.2% to $400,000, while active inventory rose 7.2% from a year ago to 842,242 single family homes.

Regionally, existing home sales fell in all-four regions compared to a year prior. The Northeast, which reported an annual sales rate of 470,000 existing homes, recorded the smallest decline at 4.1%, while the West (650,000 existing homes) reported the largest at 14.5%. On a month-over-month basis, the sales pace remained unchanged in the Northeast, but fell in the West (-9.7%), South (-6.5%) and the Midwest (-3.2%). The South reported an annual sales pace of 1.73 million homes in October, while the Midwest recorded a pace of 900,000 existing homes.

At the end of the month, there were 1.59 million existing homes for sale, up 1.3% from a month prior and 4.6% from a year ago. This inventory represents 5.1 months’ supply at the current sales pace.

Despite the slower market conditions, the median sales price continued to rise, jumping 0.4% year-over-year in October to $416,700. This marks the 40th consecutive month of year-over-year price increases, according to NAR.

Reflecting the slower pace of home sales, the median time on market for properties rose to 35 days, up from 33 days a month ago and 34 days a year ago, according to NAR’s Realtors Confidence Index.

NAR also reported that just 29% of sales were first-time buyers, down from 31% a month ago and 32% a year ago. Additionally, as mortgage rates remained at an elevated level in October, the share of all cash sales rose to 29%, compared to 27% last month. However, this is on par with a year ago.

NAR’s housing affordability index also fell, dropping from 107.3 a year ago to 100.7 in October 2026, with the index posting annual declines in all four major regions. The Northeast reported the largest annual decline of 8.7% and the West recorded the smallest at 5.2%.

“Housing affordability remains a major constraint, particularly when mortgage rates rise above the 7% psychological threshold for potential buyers,” Selma Hepp, the chief economist at Cotality and a 2025 HousingWire Vanguard award winner, said in a statement. “High rates are keeping monthly payments elevated, while tariff-related construction costs are adding pressure to insurance premiums and property taxes are further straining homeowners. Rising everyday expenses, including utility bills, are also squeezing household budgets. Together, these pressures are limiting momentum in existing-home sales.

Despite the worsening affordability challenges, just 2% of sales were distressed sales, unchanged from last month and a year ago.

Looking ahead, economists believe housing market activity will pick up if mortgage rates decline a meaningful amount.

“For housing, the key question is not simply whether mortgage rates decline, but whether they fall enough to meaningfully improve purchasing power and bring more buyers and sellers back to the market,” Hepp said. “Until then, I expect activity to remain relatively subdued, making the spring homebuying season an increasingly important test of whether the market can finally turn a corner.”

But for the next few months, industry leaders anticipate a slower pace of home sales, thanks to the level mortgage rates reached in mid-October.

“We could see further weakness, since buyers who stepped back when rates hit 7.4% in mid-October won’t show up as missing sales until November and December closings,” Sean Fergus, the vice president of economic research at NewHomeSource and Zonda, said in a statement. “Rates have since eased, but buyers need to trust that relief will last before they come back in force.”

Scout Motors has 212,000 vehicle reservations as $2 billion South Carolina plant nears completion

📅 2026-11-12  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

BLYTHEWOOD, S.C. — Electric vehicle startup Scout Motors has notched roughly 212,000 reservations for its upcoming EVs as the company nears completion of its new $2 billion plant in South Carolina, CEO Scott Keogh told CNBC.

The reservations are for the Volkswagen-backed company's Terra pickup truck and Traveler SUV, which are expected to start full production next year before deliveries begin to customers in 2028.

Reservations do not necessarily translate to sales, as the U.S. automotive industry has learned during the past decade, especially with highly anticipated all-electric trucks such as the Ford F-150 Lightning, Tesla Cybertruck and others that garnered strong interest that didn't lead to meaningful numbers of purchases.

Keogh said he expects Scout could roughly double those reservations by the time customer production begins, but he said the more important thing is to convert early interest into actual sales as production nears.

"It's been a consistent drumbeat," Keogh said during an interview at Scout's plant site outside Columbia, South Carolina. "I think we could easily be up over 400,000 with reservations without doing too much effort. The big thing for us is how much of the initial production can we 100% presell."

Keogh said achieving an order book of 12-to-18 months "would be great" and something he thinks the company can do before production. Scout on Thursday said construction is "in its final phase."

Scout's vehicles will be offered as all-electric versions as well as electric hybrid variants, known as extended-range electric vehicles, or EREVs, that include an engine as a backup generator for the electric motors and batteries.

Scout, a former American vehicle brand from 1961 to 1980, was expected to exclusively offer EVs in a bid for the German automaker to expand its presence in the U.S. However, slower-than-expected adoption of EVs and higher costs led the company to decide to offer EREVs as well.

Keogh said the EREVs and SUV are dominating reservations thus far, with roughly 87% choosing EREV models and 75% being SUVs.

The CEO also said the company is drawing interest from its target markets, which include owners of off-road vehicles such as the Ford Bronco and Jeep Wrangler as well as full-size pickup trucks and U.S. EV leader Tesla

Given the interest signaled by reservations, Keogh said the company is expected to launch with the EREVs first, followed by the EVs. He also said the company plans to launch with mid-level models first, a deviation from many companies that begin with their highest-priced models.

Both the Traveler and Terra are expected to start under $60,000. The EREV models are expected to feature more than 500 miles of range, according to the company, compared with 300 miles of range for the all-electric models.

"The No.1 thing for me is I want to go get the best volume as soon as possible," Keogh said. "In my mind, it's how quick can you get to 100,000 units? It's at 100,000 units where you can start to punch through."

His comments come months after Rivian Automotive received criticism online for launching its R2 SUV with its highest-priced models first, despite that being a common practice across the automotive industry.

Keogh said Scout's South Carolina plant is about 85% complete, but supporting infrastructure and a $300 million supplier park continue to be installed.

Reservations for Scout's vehicles initially opened in October 2024, when it revealed the Traveler and Terra, which feature sleek, yet rugged, exterior appearances that pay some homage to the brand's historic models.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/regulatory/onc-cms-launch-electronic-prior-authorization-testing-initiative" hreflang="en">ONC, CMS move electronic prior authorization into real-world testing</a>

📅 2026-11-12  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →
ONC, CMS move electronic prior authorization into real-world testing" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

The Office of the National Coordinator for Health IT (ONC) is launching a voluntary industry testing initiative to help healthcare organizations prepare for federal electronic prior authorization requirements taking effect in 2027.

ONC is collaborating with the Centers for Medicare and Medicaid Services (CMS) to launch a series of industry testing events in production-like environments to advance end-to-end electronic prior authorization (ePA), the agencies announced Thursday.

A federal rule requires some payers to implement an application programming interface (API) to support electronic prior authorization processes by January 1. Impacted payers include Medicare Advantage, Medicaid managed care, CHIP and ACA exchange plans.

The focused, 10- to 12-week testing sprints will bring together payers, providers, hospitals, electronic health record (EHR) developers, intermediaries and other industry partners to test their own workflows across connected systems and identify gaps before broader implementation, CMS and ONC officials said.

"Federal regulations and industry standards have laid important groundwork for ePA, but our work is not done," Tom Keane, M.D., National Coordinator for Health IT, said in a statement. "With our colleagues at CMS, we are bringing partners together to advance the next phase of implementation testing to accelerate ePA progress in the near term."

Organizations interested in participating can learn more and sign up for the initiative by November 20, according to ONC. Participant feedback and lessons learned will inform future testing and implementation activities.

"Regulations and industry standards have laid important groundwork for ePA but real work remains to put this policy into practice. These events mark the next phase of implementation testing, bringing trading partners together to examine end-to-end workflows in production-like environments," ONC leaders Ashley Hain and Molly Prieto, principal deputy director of the Office of Standards, Certification and Analysis, wrote in a blog post.

During the testing sprints, participants will use their own workflows to examine how prior authorization requests and decisions move across connected systems. "The focus is on finding technical barriers and workflow gaps that could arise in real-world implementation, while organizations still have an opportunity to address them before broader deployment," Hain and Prieto wrote.

ONC and CMS will use the findings from the testing events to make practical improvements. "Some findings may point to standards that need clarification or revision. Others may reveal a need for clearer educational materials for developers, providers and hospitals, or more useful implementer guidance," Hain and Prieto wrote.

"We aim to identify critical updates quickly and help industry prioritize the improvements needed for successful implementation across care settings. The purpose is to turn the progress already made through regulations and standards into workflows that reduce administrative burden and support timely care," the ONC leaders wrote.

The testing events will include workflows and real-world scenarios using the HL7 FHIR Da Vinci implementation guides for Coverage Requirements Discovery (CRD), Documentation Templates and Rules (DTR), and Prior Authorization Support (PAS). Each event will include coordinated scenarios, based off high-volume clinical services that require prior authorization and supporting resources to help participants test together, ONC said.

ONC and CMS are working with the MITRE-led Health Federally Funded Research and Development Center and Drummond Group to support stakeholder recruitment, readiness discussions, participant matching and testing coordination. Drummond Group will operate FHIRplace, the virtual environment where developers, providers, payers and intermediaries will test with their trading partners.

CMS and ONC encouraged participation from organizations of all sizes, including smaller organizations and those serving rural communities.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/providers/brigham-and-womens-nurses-authorize-open-ended-strike" hreflang="en">Brigham and Women's avoids 4,000-nurse strike with 11th hour deal</a>

📅 2026-11-12  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →
Brigham and Women's avoids 4,000-nurse strike with 11th hour deal" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

The Massachusetts Nurses Association and Mass General Brigham announced late Wednesday that their negotiating teams have reached a tentative deal, averting a potentially historic strike by 4,000 Brigham and Women's Hospital nurses by hours.

The open-ended demonstration was scheduled to begin Thursday morning, and had previously been postponed to give union members time to vote on a prior "last, best offer" from the health system, which was rejected. Their planned strike would have been the largest nurses strike in Massachusetts' history.

“I’m incredibly proud of the Brigham nurses who stood strong to protect the world-class patient care we provide,” Kelly Morgan, a labor and delivery nurse and chair of the BWH MNA Bargaining Committee. “We secured this agreement with the help of countless community members, patients, unions and elected officials who supported us for many months. By standing together, we protected patients and the future of union nursing care at the Brigham.”

The bargaining committee said it would recommend members vote to ratify the agreement, which they said addresses "the hospital's reliance" on staffing contingencies like temporary nurses and floating, maintains "affordable" health insurance benefits and new steps at the top of the hospital's wage scale for experienced nurses. Specific highlights on these and other discussion points are outlined in the union's announcement.

Brigham and Women's Hospital said it was pleased with the tentative deal, which it noted was "within the financial limits" of the prior deal it had presented last month.

"We deeply value our Brigham nurses and believe this agreement recognizes their important contributions while supporting the long-term sustainability of our hospital and our ability to care for our patients and communities," the hospital said in a statement. "We are also grateful to the nursing leaders, clinicians and staff across Mass General Brigham who worked tirelessly to ensure we were prepared to provide safe, high-quality care under any circumstance.”

In prior statements, the hospital had said it had been onboarding at least 800 agency nurses to maintain operations, with minimal adjustments to operating hours at some ambulatory sites. The final sticking points in this week's marathon bargaining sessions, per the hospital, were not related to patient or workplace safety issues, but on areas of compensation like wages and benefits.

Of note, the negotiations landed amid an announcement from parent system Mass General Brigham that a $300 million budget shortfall had triggered a new round of job cuts affecting both clinical and operational programs, including the organization's hospital-at-home program.

Union nurses at Brigham and Women's Hospital are set to strike in the coming weeks after failed contract negotiations, in what could be the largest open-ended nurse strike in Massachusetts history.

Brigham and Women's Hospital said in a statement it received notice from the Massachusetts Nurses Association (MNA) on Nov. 1 about an open-ended strike beginning Thursday, Nov. 12 at 7 a.m.

"While we had hoped to avoid another strike, we are prepared to continue providing safe, high-quality care throughout any work stoppage, building on our experience and extensive contingency planning," the statement read. "We deeply value and respect our Brigham nurses and remain committed to reaching a fair and responsible agreement. Our latest offer provides market-leading compensation and competitive benefits while recognizing our responsibility to our patients, our entire workforce and the long-term financial sustainability of Brigham and Women’s Hospital."

Negotiations with MNA are set to resume Nov. 9, the hospital said.

MNA said in a statement negotiations will focus on "securing a fair contract that meets the needs of the nurses, their patients and the community members they serve." Nurses are seeking the following terms as part of a fair contract, per the union:

"We are ready to strike but are equally prepared to negotiate a fair contract that protects patient care, respects nurses, and averts the strike," said Kelly Morgan, chair of the union's bargaining committee for the hospital. “Brigham nurses are resolved to fight for as long as it takes for patient safety and the professional respect we deserve.”

Union nurses at Brigham and Women's Hospital voted to reject management's "last, best and final offer" on updated contracts, potentially steering the dispute back toward what could be the largest open-ended nurse strike in Massachusetts history.

The Massachusetts Nurses Association had previously scheduled the strike for Oct. 14, but postponed to give its members a chance to vote on the proposal from Mass General Brigham, the hospital's parent system. The union said it has requested to resume contract negotiations this week, with the hospital responding in its own public statement this it "is prepared to maintain uninterrupted care and respond to any steps the union may take."

MGB's rejected offer would have retained the hospital's existing nursing wage progression system of annual 5% step increases until 20 years tenure, with the addition of two additional 2.5% increases at the top of the prior structure. The union has said it is seeking limits on the use of temporary nurses, more affordable health insurance benefits and “a meaningful cost-of-living wage increase” that includes a differential for nurses floating between units.

“This vote shows the resolve of Brigham nurses, who are fighting for patient safety and for the professional respect we deserve," Kelly Morgan, chair of the union's bargaining committee for the hospital, said in a weekend release. "It’s [time for] MGB to return to the table ready to negotiate in good faith to reach a fair agreement that recognizes the value of Brigham nurses and the expertise they offer for the care of some of the region’s most complex patients every day.”

Brigham and Women's Hospital said it was "disappointed" by the rejection, and that its proposal "reflected the hospital’s commitment to investing in our nurses while supporting long-term sustainability, including meaningful compensation and benefit enhancements that kept Brigham nursing pay among the highest in the market."

Notably, the announcement of the member vote's outcome coincided with news that MGB has begun a new round of layoffs affecting both clinical and operational programs. The unspecified number of cuts reflect a financial need to further adjust operating MGB's structure even after roughly 1,500 people were laid off in 2024, the system said.

Nurses at Brigham and Women’s Hospital have lifted their notice of an Oct. 14 strike after their union and Mass General Brigham management made progress toward a potential contract deal late Saturday night, the sides said in statements.

The 4,000-member open-ended strike would be the largest in state history if launched, and follows months of contract negotiations between the health system and the Massachusetts Nurses Association (see that coverage below).

A formal deal has yet to be struck, though the union said it agreed to call off its 10-day strike notice "given the complexity of the agreement and the impact of a potential strike on our nurses and the community." The proposal is being presented to members for a vote, which might yield a new 10-day notice if rejected.

Brigham and Women's Hospital, in a Sunday statement, said its "last, best and final offer ... reflects significant movement on several of the union's priorities while maintaining a responsible approach to supporting nurses, patients and the long-term strength of the hospital."

It added that the offer retains the hospital's existing nursing wage progression system of annual 5% step increases until 20 years tenure, with the addition of two additional 2.5% increases at the top of the prior structure. The withdrawn notice also saved the hospital from having to implement its contingency staffing plan of hiring temporary nurses.

"We are thankful the MNA has pulled its indefinite strike notice and will allow its members to vote on the hospital's final offer," the hospital said. The union's statement did not specify the timing of that vote.

Editor's note: On Oct. 2, the Massachusetts Nurses Association announced an Oct. 14, 7:00 a.m. start time for their strike if no deal is reached.

Nurses at Brigham and Women’s Hospital have voted to authorize an open-ended, 4,000-member strike that could become the largest nurses’ strike in Massachusetts history.

The Massachusetts Nurses Association announced the 93% in favor vote that was held Thursday evening. No date has been set yet by the union, which is legally required to give employer Mass General Brigham (MGB) 10 days' notice.

“Brigham nurses have made clear that we are prepared to stay on strike for as long as it takes to win a contract that protects our patients and respects the nurses who care for them,” Kelly Morgan, a labor and delivery nurse and chair of the Brigham and Women’s Hospital MNA Bargaining Committee, said in the union’s announcement. “An open-ended strike is a serious step that will undoubtedly create a healthcare crisis in Massachusetts. MGB can prevent the strike by returning to the table and negotiating the contract our nurses need to keep providing world-class care.”

Contract negotiations between the sides have been ongoing since last November, with the prior agreement expiring on March 31. In July, the nurses (plus an additional 450 home care clinicians) conducted a one-day awareness strike that was also a record breaker for the state and the first at Brigham and Women’s, a flagship hospital and founding member of the nonprofit system.

Demonstrations have continued in the weeks since, including a rally held earlier this week outside of an MGB-hosted World Medical Innovation Forum, where bargaining members painted a contrast between executives’ conversations with investors on AI and the demands of their clinical workforce.

The hospital, in statements given to press, said “we remain committed to bargaining in good faith and have put forward a fair and responsible proposal that maintains Brigham nurses’ market-leading compensation, preserves meaningful wage growth and competitive benefits, and balances those investments with the long-term financial sustainability of our system,” the hospital said in a statement given to press.

The hospital has previously said it has found agreements with the union on several issues. However, the union has previously said that talks stalled in mid-August and reiterated Thursday that it still is seeking limits on the use of temporary nurses, more affordable health insurance benefits and “a meaningful cost-of-living wage increase” that includes a differential for nurses floating between units.

MGB is the largest private employer in Massachusetts with roughly 85,000 workers and $22.8 billion in annual revenue, making it among the country’s largest nonprofit health systems. Financial results released last month outlined a $15 million operating income (0.1% operating margin) across nine months of its ongoing fiscal year, up from a $44 million operating loss (-0.3% operating margin), though its nine-month bottom line including nonoperating gains like investment returns was $1.9 billion. Early last year saw the organization undertake the largest layoffs in its history, when it cut about 1,500 workers.

The union has pointed to MGB’s bottom line (nearly $2.4 billion across fiscal 2025), substantial total assets ($35.8 billion) and the compensation of executives (almost $9.2 million for CEO Anne Klibanski, M.D.) in its calls for stronger contracts.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/finance/norwest-closes-4b-global-multi-stage-fund-fuel-investments-healthcare-biotech-and-ai" hreflang="en">Norwest closes $4B global, multi-stage fund to fuel investments in healthcare, biotech and AI</a>

📅 2026-11-12  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →
Norwest closes $4B global, multi-stage fund to fuel investments in healthcare, biotech and AI" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Norwest closed a $4 billion global, multi-stage fund to continue backing technology, healthcare and consumer companies across the U.S., India and Israel.

The fund, Norwest 18, the largest in the firm's 65-year history, brings the total capital under management to $18 billion, the investment firm announced Thursday. Norwest is targeting investment opportunities in healthcare, biotech, medtech and healthcare AI.

Recent investments in healthcare and biotech AI companies include patient experience platform Courier Health and Infinx. In October, the investment firm co-led Matchpoint Therapeutics' $150 million series B round, and it backed a $31 million round for Vitalize Care, an AI-powered operating system for hospital staffing and scheduling.

Since 1961, Norwest has invested in more than 750 companies and currently partners with more than 275 companies across its venture and growth equity portfolio, the company said. The firm invests in early- to late-stage businesses across key sectors with a focus on technology, healthcare and consumer.

Since the launch of Norwest 17 in April 2024, the firm has made more than 87 investments and worked with over 30 portfolio companies to achieve liquidity events. These include initial public offerings from Amagi Media Labs, BillionToOne, MBX Biosciences, Omada Health and Swiggy, among others. Notable strategic acquisitions include Avetta by EQT, AbsenceSoft by Luminate Capital Partners, Ouro Medicines by Gilead Sciences, Qualified by Salesforce, tvScientific by Pinterest and Veza by ServiceNow.

"Building and scaling a company has never been a linear journey, and today that journey is becoming more complex," said Jeff Crowe, senior managing partner at Norwest, in a statement. "Companies are staying private longer, scaling across markets, and navigating critical decisions around technology, talent, and growth. Capital is only one part of what they need. Our role is to be a long-term partner and bring the experience, networks, and resources that can help leadership teams make those decisions with confidence."

Norwest continues to expand into areas such as next-generation financial infrastructure, physical and voice AI, agentic cybersecurity, technology-enabled industrial and aerospace and healthcare AI. Recent investments include Beltic, Rain and Stablecore, companies building the next generation of financial infrastructure and advancing the adoption of digital currencies as well as Coval, Generalist AI and LlamaIndex, companies building intelligent AI systems, enabling agents to reason, interact with data and take action across digital and physical environments. Biotech and healthcare portfolio companies include Rezo Therapeutics, an AI-enabled precision medicine and biopharmaceutical company and Tokaido Health, an AI-native medication steerage platform.

The investment firm says it has expanded its support for portfolio companies beyond funding, adding more advisors, operating executives and programs focused on technology, go-to-market strategy and organizational growth.
